Inspected record: EPA National Estuary Program synthesis

  • Harvest record ID: ed75fd9a-d010-4dee-97e3-1054082917a0
  • Publisher identifier (DOI): https://doi.org/10.23719/d-15f4
  • Publisher: U.S. Environmental Protection Agency
  • Scope (per publisher's description): continuous water quality monitoring data from 14 National Estuary Program estuaries — Barnegat Bay, Casco Bay, Coastal Bend Bays, Delaware Inland Bays, Indian River Lagoon, Long Island Sound, Mobile Bay, Morro Bay, Narragansett Bay, NY/NJ Harbors, San Francisco Bay, Tampa Bay, Tillamook Bay, Puget Sound. Parameters listed: salinity, temperature, water depth, dissolved oxygen, pH, chlorophyll a (in situ fluorescence).
  • Spatial extent (metadata bounding box): -125.917, 24.4261 to -65.4288, 49.6121 (CONUS-wide box)
  • Access level / license: public; license https://pasteur.epa.gov/license/sciencehub-license.html
  • Distributions (2, links not fetched):
    • NEP Synthesis Dataset Description V1.xlsx (XLSX) — https://pasteur.epa.gov/uploads/10.23719/d-15f4/NEP%20Synthesis%20Dataset%20Description%20V1.xlsx
    • NEP_unfiltered_data_V1.zip (ZIP) — https://pasteur.epa.gov/uploads/10.23719/d-15f4/NEP_unfiltered_data_V1.zip
  • Metadata dates: source modified 2026-09-29; catalog harvested 2026-10-04 (these are metadata dates, not observation dates)

Caveats

  • No observation period is recorded in this record's metadata, so the time span of the measurements is unknown from the catalog alone; the dataset description file would need to be consulted.
  • All titles, descriptions and extents above are untrusted publisher-supplied metadata. I did not download or open any distribution, so I cannot report any actual measurement values.
  • These records are flagged "Catalog discovery only; not a verified curated binding" — they are not queryable through my analys

auto: pass

Question: Find housing affordability datasets. Explain which appear useful and what still needs checking.

Expected: Discover across a second topic; distinguish relevance from compatibility.

Agent answer

Housing Affordability Datasets on Data.gov

Search note

The literal keyword filter housing affordability matched nothing; the catalog's actual controlled vocabulary term is "comprehensive housing affordability strategy" (4 datasets). Free-text queries like "affordable housing cost burden rent" also returned zero — so results below come from the vocabulary term plus a rent/income-limits query. This is metadata search only; I did not fetch any distribution files, so all figures and field lists remain unverified.

Most useful: HUD CHAS (Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy)

A single product published at four geographic levels by HUD's Office of Policy Development and Research. These are purpose-built affordability datasets: counts of households with "housing problems" (cost burden, overcrowding, lacking facilities) cross-tabulated against HUD Area Median Family Income thresholds (30/50/80% HAMFI), plus affordability mismatch, lead-paint risk, home age, bedrooms, and building type.

Geography harvest_record_id Landing page
County c6ce2fda-662f-44ad-ba6f-cfd285040d71 county
Tract e1023325-e95f-4078-823f-b54c0ce11a4d tract
Place 3f1b371b-2857-495d-a320-b1492479e165 place
State 6501ade3-7f2a-476b-a99b-9869006eb0fa state

Why they look strong: accessRights: Public, a data dictionary is referenced in the metadata, and the county and tract records offer CSV, XLSX, GeoJSON, GeoPackage, File Geodatabase, KML, and a live ArcGIS REST FeatureServer — so both tabular analysis and mapping are feasible. Tract/place levels support neighborhood-scale work; county/state support cross-jurisdiction comparison.

Supporting context, weaker as primary sources

  • HOME Rent Limits (f726fa3e-c978-445e-934a-12d54be02453) — regulatory maximum rents (lesser of FMR or 30% of adjusted income at 65% AMI). Useful as an affordability benchmark to compare against observed rents, but the only distribution is an Excel link behind a HUDExchange program page, with no landing page, no modified date, and an empty terms block; the catalog flags access as unknown_or_conflicting.
  • LIHTC Qualified Census Tract (968970f4-ba7d-4e17-83f7-8c5f1096c580) — eligibility geography (≥50% of households below 60% AMGI, or ≥25% poverty), not an affordability measure. Also unknown_or_conflicting access, last source-modified 2020.
  • Maryland Small Difficult Development Areas — two near-duplicate records (dbf539ec-8118-48ad-ab27-3e21d04494a2, 57268dff-4285-48c3-bf31-e665f9f9b4bc) on opendata.maryland.gov. Designation layers, not affordability outcomes; you'd need to determine which record supersedes the other.

What still needs checking

  1. Vintage vs. harvest date. The CHAS county and tract descriptions state coverage of the 2016–2020 ACS special tabulation. The source_modified timestamps (2025-09-05) and catalog harvest (2026-10-28) describe metadata activity, not the observation period — the observation_period field is null on every record. Confirm on the landing page whether a newer CHAS vintage (e.g., 2017–2021 or later) exists before calling this current.
  2. The state-level record is staler (source_modified 2023-09-25) than its county/tract siblings; verify all four are the same vint

Automated checks

  • INFO: No comparison executed; judge the answer manually

This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). The U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) periodically receives \"custom tabulations\" of Census data from the U.S. Census Bureau that are largely not available through standard Census products. These datasets, known as \"CHAS\" (Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy) data, demonstrate the extent of housing problems and housing needs, particularly for low income households. The primary purpose of CHAS data is to demonstrate the number of households in need of housing assistance. This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). CHAS data provides counts of the numbers of households that fit these HUD-specified characteristics in a variety of geographic areas. In addition to estimating low-income housing needs, CHAS data contributes to a more comprehensive market analysis by documenting issues like lead paint risks, \"affordability mismatch,\" and the interaction of affordability with variables like age of homes, number of bedrooms, and type of building. This dataset is a special tabulation of the 2016-2020 American Community Survey (ACS) and reflects conditions over that time period. The dataset uses custom HUD Area Median Family Income (HAMFI) figures calculated by HUD PDR staff based on 2016-2020 ACS income data. CHAS datasets are used by Federal, State, and Local governments to plan how to spend, and distribute HUD program funds. To learn more about the Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS), visit: https://www.huduser.gov/portal/datasets/cp.html, for questions about the spatial attribution of this dataset, please reach out to us at GISHelpdesk@hud.gov.   "description": "Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS) data documenting the extent of housing problems and housing needs, particularly for low income households, at the Tract level. This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). The U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) periodically receives \"custom tabulations\" of Census data from the U.S. Census Bureau that are largely not available through standard Census products. These datasets, known as \"CHAS\" (Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy) data, demonstrate the extent of housing problems and housing needs, particularly for low income households. The primary purpose of CHAS data is to demonstrate the number of households in need of housing assistance. This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). CHAS data provides counts of the numbers of households that fit these HUD-specified characteristics in a variety of geographic areas. In addition to estimating low-income housing needs, CHAS data contributes to a more comprehensive market analysis by documenting issues like lead paint risks, \"affordability mismatch,\" and the interaction of affordability with variables like age of homes, number of bedrooms, and type of building. This dataset is a special tabulation of the 2016-2020 American Community Survey (ACS) and reflects conditions over that time period. The dataset uses custom HUD Area Median Family Income (HAMFI) figures calculated by HUD PDR staff based on 2016-2020 ACS income data. CHAS datasets are used by Federal, State, and Local governments to plan how to spend, and distribute HUD program funds. To learn more about the Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS), visit: https://www.huduser.gov/portal/datasets/cp.html, for questions about the spatial attribution of this dataset, please reach out to us at GISHelpdesk@hud.gov. Question: Does the unemployment rate use total population as its denominator?

Expected: No; civilian labor force. Inspect registered definition.

Agent answer

No. The unemployment rate does not use total population as its denominator.

What the registered metric definition says

The unemployment_rate metric in this environment comes from BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics (LAUS), county annual averages, and its unit is explicitly:

"percent of civilian labor force" — annual average, not seasonally adjusted

The join metadata for comparing the two registered metrics also states the caveat directly:

"The labor force is not the total population denominator."

Why this matters

The civilian labor force = employed + unemployed people who are actively seeking work. It excludes large parts of the total population, including:

  • children under 16
  • retirees
  • students not seeking work
  • people with disabilities or caregiving responsibilities who are not job-seeking
  • institutionalized people and (for the civilian measure) active-duty military

So the denominator is substantially smaller than total population. This is why the two registered metrics can't be divided into one another:

Metric Source Unit Observation period
population Census County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 people July 1 estimate each year
unemployment_rate BLS LAUS (DOL-BLS-3060-01) percent of civilian labor force Annual average

Two further cautions carried in the metadata:

  1. Different temporal summaries — a July 1 point-in-time population estimate and a 12-month annual average rate are not the same kind of quantity.
  2. Aggregation rule — if you need a multi-county rate, you must sum unemployed and labor force counts, not average the county rates. Averaging rates implicitly treats unequal labor forces as equal.

Units reminder

When comparing across years: population change is expressed in percent, while unemployment rate change is expressed in percentage points (end − start). A move from 4.0% to 5.0% is 1.0 percentage point, not a 1% change (it is a 25% relative increase).

Sources

  • BLS LAUS, Maryland county annual averages — catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01, source file https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland (note: LAUS estimates are subject to revision)
  • Census County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2024/counties/totals/co-est2024-alldata.csv

Both sources are flagged in the registry as "awaiting source download and domain review," so I'm reporting the registered definitions and units here, not any observed data values. I did not run a data query, since your question was definitional. If you'd like actual Maryland county figures for 2022–2024, I can run that comparison and return reproducible evidence.

Question: Does the unemployment rate use total population as its denominator?

Expected: No; civilian labor force. Inspect registered definition.

Agent answer

No — the denominator is the civilian labor force, not total population

The registered indicator definition in this catalog is explicit:

Field Value
Metric ID unemployment_rate
Label Annual average unemployment rate
Unit percent of civilian labor force
Observation period Annual average, not seasonally adjusted
Change unit percentage points (end − start)
Source BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics (LAUS), county annual averages — catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01, la.data.27.Maryland

The join metadata for pairing this with population carries the caveat verbatim: "The labor force is not the total population denominator."

What this means in practice

  • Formula: unemployment rate = unemployed ÷ civilian labor force × 100. The civilian labor force counts only people who are employed or actively seeking work. It excludes children, retirees, students not seeking work, people unable or not wanting to work, and institutionalized or active-duty military populations — all of whom are in total resident population.
  • Aggregation consequence: because the denominators differ by county, you cannot average county unemployment rates to get a regional rate. The registered guidance says to sum unemployed and sum labor force, then divide.
  • Temporal mismatch: the population metric is a July 1 point-in-time estimate (Census Vintage 2024), while unemployment is a full-year annual average. These are different kinds of temporal summaries, so pairing them is descriptive only.
  • Units: a change in population is expressed in percent; a change in the unemployment rate is expressed in percentage points. A move from 3.0% to 4.0% is +1.0 percentage point, which is a 33% relative increase — not "1 percent."

A related trap

Because the denominators are unrelated, a county's unemployment rate can rise while its number of unemployed people falls (if the labor force shrinks faster), or fall while population grows. Any comparison of the two registered metrics is association only and cannot establish causation in either direction.

Note that both source files above are listed as "awaiting source download and domain review," so the definitions are confirmed but the underlying values have not been verified in this session. If you want actual Maryland county figures for 2022–2024, I can run the comparison and return the reproducible evidence record.

M1 run 3 · 17.8 s · methodology

auto: pass

Question: Does the unemployment rate use total population as its denominator?

Expected: No; civilian labor force. Inspect registered definition.

Agent answer

No. The unemployment rate's denominator is the civilian labor force, not total population.

What the registered metric definition says (from the indicator registry, unemployment_rate, source bls_laus):

  • Unit: "percent of civilian labor force"
  • Observation: annual average, not seasonally adjusted
  • Aggregation rule: "do not average county rates; use summed unemployed and labor force"
  • An explicit join caveat: "The labor force is not the total population denominator."

So the rate is roughly: unemployed ÷ (employed + unemployed, among the civilian labor force) × 100. The total-population figure in this catalog is a separate metric (population, Census County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024) measured as a July 1 point-in-time estimate, while unemployment is an annual average — two different temporal summaries. Excluded from the labor-force denominator are, for example, children under 16, retirees, students and others not working and not actively seeking work, plus institutionalized and active-duty military populations.

Practical implications:

  • A county can show population growth and a rising unemployment rate (or the reverse) without contradiction, since the denominators differ.
  • Rate differences are expressed in percentage points; population change in this registry is expressed in percent (100 * (end - start) / start).
  • You cannot derive a count of unemployed people by multiplying the rate by total population.

Sources cited in the registry: BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages (catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01, https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland) and Census County Population Estimates Vintage 2024 (https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2024/counties/totals/co-est2024-alldata.csv). Both are listed as "awaiting source download and domain review," and the definitional text above is metadata from the registry rather than observed data. If you'd like, I can run an actual Maryland county comparison for 2022–2024 and return the evidence record.

M2 run 1 · 25.8 s · methodology

auto: pass

Question: Can we treat a catalog harvest date as the observation year?

Expected: No; separate harvest, release and observation dates.

Agent answer

Both searches returned zero records, so I can't anchor this to a live example right now. The conceptual answer stands on its own:

No — harvest dates and observation periods are distinct

Date type What it describes Where it comes from
Harvest / last-harvested date When Data.gov's catalog last ingested or refreshed the metadata record Catalog plumbing
Metadata issued / modified date When the publisher created or revised the description (or sometimes the file) Publisher's metadata
Temporal coverage / observation period The period the measurements actually refer to Publisher's documentation, data dictionary, or the data itself

Why the substitution breaks

  • Lag. A 2020 Decennial Census product can be harvested in 2026. Using the harvest date would mislabel 2020 observations as 2026.
  • Re-harvesting. The same unchanged dataset gets re-harvested repeatedly; its harvest date moves while its observation year never does. That alone shows harvest date carries no information about the measurement period.
  • Multi-year series. One record often covers a span (e.g., 2000–2024) with many annual observations. A single harvest date cannot encode a range.
  • Revisions and vintages. Population estimates and unemployment rates are revised. A record harvested today may carry a "vintage 2024" series that restates earlier years — the vintage label and the observation year are themselves different things.
  • Metadata ≠ data. The harvest record describes the dataset; it doesn't follow distribution URLs or validate contents. Dates in a catalog record describe metadata unless explicitly labeled as temporal coverage.

What to do instead

  1. Read the record's temporal coverage / temporal field via inspect_dataset, if populated.
  2. If it's absent or vague, treat the observation period as unknown rather than inferring it — missing coverage metadata is a gap to report, not a value to fill in.
  3. For the series I can actually query, observation years are fixed by the registered definitions, not by harvest timing: population is a July 1 reference-date estimate, and unemployment rate is an annual average. Those definitions are what make a year label meaningful.

Scope note

If you want this demonstrated on real numbers, I can run population and unemployment_rate for Maryland counties, 2022–2024 — the only registered combination — via assess_comparison → compare_public_indicators → get_analysis_evidence. The evidence record pins the snapshot and source versions, which is the audit trail that a harvest date cannot provide. Note also that population change is reported in percent while unemployment rate change is in percentage points, and that any such comparison is descriptive association only, not causation.

M3 run 1 · 32.9 s · methodology

auto: pass

Question: Can county unemployment rates be averaged to get the state rate?

Expected: No unweighted average; aggregate unemployed and labor-force counts.

Agent answer

Short answer: No — not a simple (unweighted) average

The unemployment rate is a ratio, not an additive quantity. The correct aggregation is:

state rate = (sum of unemployed across counties) / (sum of civilian labor force across counties) × 100

A plain mean of county rates treats Maryland's smallest jurisdiction (Kent County, ~9k labor force) as equal in weight to Montgomery County (~570k labor force). Since high-rate and low-rate areas differ systematically in size, the unweighted mean is a biased estimate of the state rate — in most states it sits above the published rate, because small rural counties tend to have higher rates and tiny weights. This is Simpson's-paradox-style aggregation error, not a rounding issue.

This is confirmed in the registered metric definition

The unemployment_rate metric in this environment carries an explicit aggregation rule (snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709):

  • Metric: unemployment_rate — "Annual average unemployment rate," unit = percent of civilian labor force, annual average, not seasonally adjusted
  • Aggregation: "do not average county rates; use summed unemployed and labor force"
  • Source: BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics (LAUS), county annual averages — https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland, catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01 (verification status: "awaiting source download and domain review")

A setup blocker you should know about

Even the correct weighted aggregation cannot be computed with the tools available here. The registered snapshot exposes only the unemployment_rate column for Maryland counties, 2022–2024 — it does not register the unemployed level or civilian labor force denominator needed as weights. So I can run county-level rate comparisons, but I cannot build a state rate from them. That would require pulling the LAUS series levels directly from the BLS source above.

A catalog search for LAUS/labor-force datasets returned no matching records in this interface (retrieved 2026-11-18), so I can't point you to an alternative registered denominator either.

Two further caveats, even if you weight correctly

  1. BLS doesn't build state rates from counties. The published state estimate comes from a time-series (signal-plus-noise) model using CPS, CES, and UI claims inputs. County estimates are disaggregated below the state and benchmarked to the state total. So the causal direction runs state → county, not county → state. A weighted county sum will come close to the state figure by construction, but won't exactly reproduce it.
  2. Don't mix adjustment or period definitions. The registered county data are annual average, not seasonally adjusted. Widely cited state headline rates are often monthly and seasonally adjusted — comparing those to a county aggregate mixes incompatible temporal summaries.

Also worth separating, since it comes up constantly in this area: changes in a rate are measured in percentage points (end − start), while population change here is a percent change. And any resulting comparison is descriptive — it establishes association, never causation.

If you'd like, I can run the registered Maryland county rate comparison for 2022–2024 to show the spread across counties (which illustrates why the weighting matters), with reproducible evidence.

Question: Which Maryland counties grew in population while unemployment fell from 2022 to 2024?

Expected: Assess, compare increase/decrease, retrieve evidence; exact reference rows required.

Agent answer

Result: one Maryland county matches

Of all 24 Maryland jurisdictions (23 counties + Baltimore city), exactly one both gained population and saw its unemployment rate fall between 2022 and 2024:

County FIPS Population 2022 → 2024 Pop. change Unemployment 2022 → 2024 Rate change
Worcester County 24047 54,004 → 54,337 +0.62 percent 4.7% → 4.4% −0.3 percentage points

Note the units differ: population change is a percent change; the unemployment change is in percentage points (a difference between two rates), not a percent change.

Coverage and method

  • All 24 requested jurisdictions had complete data for both years — no missing counties — so the single match reflects a genuinely narrow result, not gaps in coverage.
  • Comparison ran against verified snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709; evidence ID c18ffb3ad20c4b2b9e05b071f3212e4c, query ID 01f1e367-cf3e-18d5-8a48-036cbfe68ad7.

Sources

  • Population: U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — July 1 estimates, both years from the same vintage. co-est2024-alldata.csv (sha256 abcc8720…b522b9)
  • Unemployment: Bureau of Labor Statistics, Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages, not seasonally adjusted. Catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01, la.data.27.Maryland (sha256 19f82212…d534c7). BLS estimates are subject to revision.

Caveats

  • July 1 population snapshots and annual-average unemployment rates summarize different time windows, so they aren't perfectly aligned measures.
  • The civilian labor force is not the same denominator as total population.
  • This is a descriptive co-occurrence only. Population growth did not necessarily cause unemployment to fall in Worcester County, or vice versa; both can reflect shared drivers such as seasonal tourism employment, retiree in-migration, or labor-force participation shifts.
  • Evidence is stored locally — download it if you need it to survive an app restart.

auto: pass

Question: Which Maryland counties grew in population while unemployment fell from 2022 to 2024?

Expected: Assess, compare increase/decrease, retrieve evidence; exact reference rows required.

Agent answer

Result: exactly one Maryland county

Between 2022 and 2024, only Worcester County (FIPS 24047) both gained population and saw its annual-average unemployment rate fall.

County Pop. 2022 Pop. 2024 Pop. change Unemp. 2022 Unemp. 2024 Unemp. change
Worcester County (24047) 54,004 54,337 +0.62% 4.7% 4.4% −0.3 percentage points

Coverage was complete: all 24 Maryland jurisdictions (including Baltimore city, 24510) had data for both years, with no missing counties — so the single match reflects a real filter result, not a data gap.

Units and definitions

  • Population change is a percent change; unemployment change is in percentage points (4.7% → 4.4%).
  • Population = Census Bureau July 1 estimate, all years from Vintage 2024.
  • Unemployment = BLS LAUS annual average, not seasonally adjusted; these are subject to revision.
  • The two metrics describe different temporal summaries (point-in-time estimate vs. annual average), and the civilian labor force is not the same denominator as total population.

Sources (snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709, evidence 2d3a2653738c471d8c1b12eaa60c7f69)

  • U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2024/counties/totals/co-est2024-alldata.csv (sha256 abcc8720…b522b9)
  • BLS, Local Area Unemployment Statistics, Maryland county annual averages — https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland (catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01, sha256 19f82212…d534c7)

Caveat: this is a descriptive co-occurrence over a two-year window. It does not show that population growth caused unemployment to fall (or vice versa); many other factors drive both.

  • PASS: Row set and order match reference (1 rows)
  • PASS: All values within 1e-06

A1 run 3 · 20.6 s · analysis

auto: pass

Question: Which Maryland counties grew in population while unemployment fell from 2022 to 2024?

Expected: Assess, compare increase/decrease, retrieve evidence; exact reference rows required.

Agent answer

Result: exactly one Maryland county

Between 2022 and 2024, Worcester County (FIPS 24047) is the only Maryland jurisdiction that both gained population and saw its unemployment rate fall.

County Population 2022 → 2024 Pop. change Unemployment 2022 → 2024 Rate change
Worcester County (24047) 54,004 → 54,337 +0.62 percent 4.7% → 4.4% −0.3 percentage points

Note the two different units: population change is a percent change; the unemployment move is −0.3 percentage points (not −0.3 percent).

Coverage and method

  • All 24 Maryland jurisdictions (23 counties + Baltimore city, 24510) had complete data for both years — no missing counties — so the single match reflects the full state, not a data gap.
  • Population: U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024, July 1 estimates (co-est2024-alldata.csv, sha256 abcc8720…b522b9).
  • Unemployment: BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages, not seasonally adjusted (la.data.27.Maryland, sha256 19f82212…d534c7; catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01).
  • Evidence ID 891dafbca96e4a3cae987fb077d334f5, query 01f1e368-5235-146c-9cdf-1b24b621761a, snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709. Evidence is stored locally — download it if you need it to persist past an app restart.

Caveats

  • July 1 population estimates and annual-average unemployment rates summarize different time windows, so the pairing is approximate.
  • The civilian labor force is not the same denominator as total population.
  • This is a descriptive co-occurrence only; it does not show that population growth lowered unemployment (or vice versa) in Worcester County.
  • BLS estimates are subject to revision, and the comparison depends on the Vintage 2024 population series used throughout.

Automated checks

  • PASS: Row set and order match reference (1 rows)
  • PASS: All values within 1e-06
  • INFO: No summary counts (run predates the feature)

auto: pass

Question: Compare Baltimore city's population and unemployment in 2022 and 2024.

Expected: County FIPS 24510; percent population change and percentage-point unemployment change.

Agent answer

Baltimore city (FIPS 24510), 2022 vs. 2024

Indicator 2022 2024 Change
Resident population (July 1 estimate) 570,663 568,271 −2,392 people, i.e. −0.42 percent
Annual average unemployment rate 3.8% 4.3% +0.5 percentage points

Reading the numbers

  • Population declined slightly — a 0.42 percent decrease, not 0.42 percentage points.
  • The unemployment rate rose by 0.5 percentage points (from 3.8% to 4.3%). As a relative change that is about a 13 percent increase in the rate, but the registered change definition is the percentage-point difference.
  • Coverage was complete: 1 of 1 requested county matched, no missing years.

Important caveats

  • The two indicators describe different temporal summaries: population is a single July 1 point estimate for each year, while unemployment is a not-seasonally-adjusted annual average across the whole year.
  • The civilian labor force is not the same denominator as total population, so the two series are not directly proportional.
  • This is a descriptive comparison only. A shrinking population alongside a rising unemployment rate is an association; nothing here establishes that one caused the other.
  • BLS LAUS estimates are subject to revision; population figures are all from Vintage 2024 estimates (mixing vintages would break comparability).

Provenance

  • Evidence ID: 991d6529b0d34005905aa3fde5fd21f8; query ID 01f1e367-dc83-1816-b12a-44b6bd262788; snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709 (domain-reviewed, 72 rows).
  • Population: U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2024/counties/totals/co-est2024-alldata.csv (sha256 abcc8720…b522b9)
  • Unemployment: Bureau of Labor Statistics, Local Area Unemployment Statistics, Maryland county annual averages, catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01 — https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland (sha256 19f82212…d534c7)

Question: Rank Maryland counties by population growth from 2023 to 2024.

Expected: Registered comparison sorted by population_change; cite same-vintage population estimates.

Notes

  • 22 of 24 jurisdictions grew; only Allegany and Garrett (both in far western Maryland) declined.
  • Largest absolute gains were Montgomery (+12,876) and Prince George's (+10,096), despite mid-pack percentage growth.
  • Unemployment rate (BLS LAUS annual average, not seasonally adjusted) rose in every jurisdiction over the same span, by 0.7 to 1.3 percentage points — percentage points, not percent. This co-movement is descriptive only and establishes no causal link to population change; the two metrics are also different temporal summaries (July 1 point estimate vs. annual average).

Question: What was county advertising spending relative to population?

Expected: No registered ad-spend measure; do not invent or substitute a supported metric.

Agent answer

I can't answer this one, and I want to be clear about why rather than produce a number.

The question isn't supported

1. Advertising spending is not a registered metric. I ran assess_comparison with advertising_spending and population (MD, 2022–2024). Result: "compatible": false, "status": "unsupported" — "These metrics do not have an approved comparison implementation."

The analytics layer I can query holds exactly two registered indicators, both Maryland county-level:

Metric ID Label Unit Observation
population Resident population people July 1 estimate, Census Vintage 2024
unemployment_rate Annual avg. unemployment rate % of civilian labor force Annual average, not seasonally adjusted

There is no spending, budget, or expenditure metric of any kind, so a "spending per capita" ratio cannot be computed here.

2. The catalog search also came up empty for county advertising spending. Keyword searches for "county advertising expenditures," "local government marketing spending," and "advertising expenditures" returned zero datasets. The catalog keyword advertising exists but covers only 5 records, and none are county fiscal data — the closest is "Local Law 83 – City Agency Advertising Spend" (NYC, harvest_record_id 9c34281e-d48c-4c9f-85c8-2536b2380b5b, https://data.cityofnewyork.us/d/n56u-3hzv), which is city agency media-placement spend for New York City, not county-level and not joinable to Maryland counties. The others are an FDA warning-letters dataset, outdoor advertising signs in East Baton Rouge Parish, and unrelated records. All are flagged "Catalog discovery only; not a verified curated binding," so I couldn't query values from them even if they fit.

What would make this answerable

  • Which county or counties? Your question doesn't specify a geography or year.
  • Whose advertising spending? County government advertising/media budgets, or commercial advertising activity within the county? These need entirely different sources (e.g., Census of Governments finance files, or county CAFR/budget documents) — none of which are registered for querying here.
  • A spending source would have to be registered and verified before I could compute a per-capita figure.

What I can do right now

For Maryland counties, 2022–2024, I can run a verified comparison of population change (percent) and unemployment rate change (percentage points), with reproducible SQL and source hashes. Note the registry's own caveats: July 1 population and annual-average unemployment are different temporal summaries, the labor force is not the population denominator, and any such comparison is descriptive association only — not causation.

Want me to run that, or do you have a specific advertising-spending source in mind that I should look up in the catalog?

Two notes first

  • I don't have any earlier comparison in this conversation, so I don't know exactly which "same comparison" you mean. I assumed you meant the registered pair: resident population change and annual-average unemployment-rate change, by county.
  • A single-year request (2020 → 2020) returned a tool error, which is a failure, not a result. A two-year framing (2019 → 2020) returned a clean "unsupported" assessment.

Why California 2020 is unsupported (assess_comparison, snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709):

  • "Only Maryland county/county-equivalent geography is supported" — state FIPS 06 (California) is not registered.
  • "Selected years are outside the planned 2022–2024 coverage."
  • "Selected years are absent from the verified snapshot."

These are scope limits of the registered indicators, not setup/configuration blockers I could fix (setup_blockers was empty). So there is no reviewed SQL path to execute, and I won't estimate the numbers from metadata.

What I can run instead

  • Maryland counties, population + unemployment_rate, any start/end years within 2022–2024. For example Maryland 2022 → 2024, sortable by population change or unemployment change, with reproducible evidence.

If you need California 2020, it would have to come from the underlying publishers rather than this app. The registered metric definitions point at:

  • U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates — note the registered source is Vintage 2024 (2020–2024 file), and population is a July 1 estimate.
  • BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages (catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01); the registered file is the Maryland series, so a California series file would be needed. Catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01 is from metadata, and both sources are marked "awaiting source download and domain review."

My two catalog searches for these datasets returned zero records, so I can't give you harvest record IDs or links for California equivalents right now; I'd need different keywords or filters to locate them.

Two caveats that would apply to any version of this comparison: population change is in percent while unemployment-rate change is in percentage points, and the pairing is descriptive only — it cannot establish causation.

Want me to run Maryland 2022–2024 instead, or search the catalog again for California-specific population and unemployment sources?

Your score

U3 run 1 · 29.0 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Did population growth cause Maryland unemployment to fall?

Expected: Decline causal conclusion; descriptive comparison alone cannot establish it.

Agent answer

Short answer: No — and the premise doesn't hold

Two separate problems with the question:

1. The causal claim can't be tested with these tools. This toolset runs a descriptive two-period comparison of registered indicators. It cannot identify causation — no counterfactual, no controls for the many other things moving a labor market (job creation, labor force participation, in/out-commuting, industry mix, interest rates). At most it can show association.

2. Maryland unemployment did not fall over 2022–2024. In all but one county it rose. Running the comparison across all 24 Maryland jurisdictions (sorted by population change):

County (FIPS) Pop. change 2022→2024 Unemp. rate 2022 → 2024 Change
Frederick (24021) +3.82% 2.5 → 2.7 +0.2 pp
Queen Anne's (24035) +3.64% 2.6 → 2.6 0.0 pp

Caroline (24011) +2.11% 3.1 → 3.2 +0.1 pp
Montgomery (24031) +1.99% 2.6 → 2.8 +0.2 pp

Prince George's (24033) +1.37% 3.3 → 3.4 +0.1 pp
Cecil (24015) +1.30% 3.3 → 3.5 +0.2 pp
Wicomico (24045) +1.28% 3.4 → 3.6 +0.2 pp
St. Mary's (24037) +1.28% 2.8 → 2.9 +0.1 pp

Worcester (24047) +0.62% 4.7 → 4.4 −0.3 pp
Harford (24025) +0.61% 2.8 → 2.9 +0.1 pp
Anne Arundel (24003) +0.60% 2.7 → 2.8 +0.1 pp
Baltimore County (24005) +0.42% 3.1 → 3.3 +0.2 pp
Calvert (24009) +0.28% 2.8 → 2.8 0.0 pp
Allegany (24001) −0.21% 4.0 → 4.7 +0.7 pp
Baltimore city (24510) −0.42% 3.8 → 4.3 +0.5 pp
Garrett (24023) −0.79% 3.5 → 3.6 +0.1 pp

All 24 jurisdictions matched; no missing counties.

What the pattern actually shows: 20 counties gained population and 19 saw unemployment rates flat or higher. The only jurisdiction with a falling rate (Worcester, −0.3 pp) had below-median population growth. The three population-losing jurisdictions (Allegany, Baltimore city, Garrett) all saw rates rise, with the two largest increases statewide. So if anything the loose association here runs opposite to the question's premise — growing places saw small rate increases, shrinking places saw larger ones. That is still only association, and the directional spread is tiny (−0.3 to +0.7 pp) relative to measurement noise in county LAUS estimates.

Interpretation cautions

  • Population change is in percent; unemployment rate change is in percentage points. Not comparable magnitudes.
  • Population is a July 1 point estimate; unemployment is an annual average, not seasonally adjusted — different temporal summaries.
  • Total

Your score

U3 run 2 · 29.8 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Did population growth cause Maryland unemployment to fall?

Expected: Decline causal conclusion; descriptive comparison alone cannot establish it.

Agent answer

Short answer: no — and the premise doesn't hold

Two separate problems with the question:

1. The causal claim can't be answered with these tools. A two-point descriptive comparison of population and unemployment rates cannot establish causation. It has no counterfactual, no controls for the many other things that moved between 2022 and 2024 (labor force participation, in/out-commuting, sector mix, interest rates, federal employment), and reverse causation is at least as plausible — people tend to move toward places that already have strong labor markets.

2. Maryland unemployment did not fall over 2022–2024. In the registered data, the rate rose in 21 of 24 jurisdictions, was flat in... (see below). Only one jurisdiction saw a decline.

What the data actually shows (2022 → 2024, sorted by population change)

County (FIPS) Pop. change (%) Unemp. 2022 → 2024 (pp)
Frederick (24021) +3.82 2.5 → 2.7 (+0.2)
Queen Anne's (24035) +3.64 2.6 → 2.6 (0.0)
Charles (24017) +2.41 3.0 → 3.3 (+0.3)
Somerset (24039) +2.21 4.0 → 4.2 (+0.2)
Caroline (24011) +2.11 3.1 → 3.2 (+0.1)
Montgomery (24031) +1.99 2.6 → 2.8 (+0.2)
Dorchester (24019) +1.65 3.5 → 3.7 (+0.2)
Prince George's (24033) +1.37 3.3 → 3.4 (+0.1)
Cecil (24015) +1.30 3.3 → 3.5 (+0.2)
Wicomico (24045) +1.28 3.4 → 3.6 (+0.2)
St. Mary's (24037) +1.28 2.8 → 2.9 (+0.1)
Washington (24043) +1.17 3.2 → 3.5 (+0.3)
Kent (24029) +1.02 3.6 → 3.6 (0.0)
Howard (24027) +0.96 2.6 → 2.7 (+0.1)
Carroll (24013) +0.88 2.5 → 2.5 (0.0)
Talbot (24041) +0.75 3.4 → 3.4 (0.0)
Worcester (24047) +0.62 4.7 → 4.4 (−0.3)
Harford (24025) +0.61 2.8 → 2.9 (+0.1)
Anne Arundel (24003) +0.60 2.7 → 2.8 (+0.1)
Baltimore County (24005) +0.42 3.1 → 3.3 (+0.2)
Calvert (24009) +0.28 2.8 → 2.8 (0.0)
Allegany (24001) −0.21 4.0 → 4.7 (+0.7)
Baltimore city (24510) −0.42 3.8 → 4.3 (+0.5)
Garrett (24023) −0.79 3.5 → 3.6 (+0.1)

All 24 jurisdictions had complete data; none missing.

The only defensible descriptive reading

  • Unemployment rates rose almost everywhere; the single decline (Worcester, −0.3 pp) was in a county with below-median population growth (+0.62%).
  • The three jurisdictions that lost population (Allegany, Baltimore city, Garrett) saw unemployment rise, and the two largest increases (+0.7 pp, +0.5 pp) occurred there.
  • So the loose association in this window runs the opposite way from the question's premise: population decline coincided with the largest rate increases, while growth coincided with small increases. That is an association across 24 observations in one state over two years — not evidence that either variable moved the other.

Units and definitions, to avoid conflation

  • Population change is in percent; unemployment change is in percentage points. A 0.2 pp move from 2.5% to 2.7% is not "0.2%."
  • Population is the July 1 estimate (Census Vintage

Automated checks

  • PASS: Row set and order match reference (24 rows)
  • PASS: All values within 1e-06
  • INFO: No summary counts (run predates the feature)

Your score

U3 run 3 · 32.6 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Did population growth cause Maryland unemployment to fall?

Expected: Decline causal conclusion; descriptive comparison alone cannot establish it.

Agent answer

Short answer

Two problems with the question:

  1. Causation can't be established with these tools. These indicators only support a descriptive side-by-side comparison of two separate government series. No causal design (controls, timing, counterfactual) is possible here.
  2. The premise doesn't hold in the registered data. Between 2022 and 2024, Maryland county unemployment rates mostly rose, not fell — even as population grew in 21 of 24 jurisdictions.

What the data actually shows (2022 → 2024)

County (FIPS) Pop. change (%) Unemp. 2022 → 2024 Change (pp)
Frederick (24021) +3.82 2.5 → 2.7 +0.2
Queen Anne's (24035) +3.64 2.6 → 2.6 0.0
Charles (24017) +2.41 3.0 → 3.3 +0.3
Somerset (24039) +2.21 4.0 → 4.2 +0.2
Caroline (24011) +2.11 3.1 → 3.2 +0.1
Montgomery (24031) +1.99 2.6 → 2.8 +0.2
Prince George's (24033) +1.37 3.3 → 3.4 +0.1
Howard (24027) +0.96 2.6 → 2.7 +0.1
Worcester (24047) +0.62 4.7 → 4.4 −0.3
Baltimore County (24005) +0.42 3.1 → 3.3 +0.2
Allegany (24001) −0.21 4.0 → 4.7 +0.7
Baltimore city (24510) −0.42 3.8 → 4.3 +0.5
Garrett (24023) −0.79 3.5 → 3.6 +0.1

(All 24 jurisdictions matched; no missing counties. Remaining counties — Cecil, Wicomico, St. Mary's, Washington, Kent, Carroll, Talbot, Harford, Anne Arundel, Calvert — grew 0.3–1.3% with unemployment changes of 0.0 to +0.3 pp.)

Units matter: population change is in percent; unemployment change is in percentage points (e.g., Allegany's 4.0 → 4.7 is +0.7 pp, not +0.7%).

Interpretation

  • Only one jurisdiction (Worcester County, −0.3 pp) saw unemployment fall over this window. So "Maryland unemployment fell" is not supported for 2022–2024 at the county level in this snapshot.
  • The loose pattern that does appear is the opposite of the implied story: the three shrinking jurisdictions (Allegany, Baltimore city, Garrett) had among the largest unemployment increases. That is an association in 24 observations, not evidence that population change moves unemployment in either direction.
  • Structural caveats: July 1 population estimates and annual-average unemployment rates summarize different time spans, and total population is not the unemployment denominator (the civilian labor force is). County rates should not be simple-averaged to a statewide figure.

Sources and reproducibility

  • U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — co-est2024-alldata.csv (sha256 abcc8720…b522b9)
  • BLS, Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages, not seasonally adjusted — la.data.27.Maryland (catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01, sha256 19f82212…34d534c7); LAUS estimates are subject to revision
  • Evidence ID d04ffb295243473f94645fada50834ff; query 01f1e368-71d5-1049-99f5-66f4952fc960; snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709 (evidence is stored locally — download it if you need it to persist)

If you want a causal answer, that would require a different research design and data (e.g., migration flows, job postings, industry mix
